and
T_{5}
are both non-empty. So we have the sets
X_{1},T_{1},T_{3},T_{5}
non-empty. Take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
G[X]
and
G[T]
. Then by Observations
,
, and
,
X_{1}
is either a join or a co-join with the three sets
T_{1},T_{3},T_{5}
, so labelling is trivial.

Therefore, the graph
G[X\cup T]
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

6.2.5
The Graph
G[T\cup Y]

Observation 6.13

The graph
G[Y_{i}\cup T_{i+1}]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. Assume
Y_{1}
and
T_{2}
are non-empty. By P
, a vertex in
Y_{1}
is non-adjacent to at most two vertices in
T_{2}
, but not necessarily vice versa. By P
, if a vertex
a\in Y_{1}
is non-adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in T_{2}
, one of
\{b,c\}
must be adjacent to all of
Y_{1}-a
while the other must be non-adjacent to all of
Y_{1}-a
. Without loss of generality, assume in this case
b
is adjacent to
Y_{1}-a
while
c
is non-adjacent to
Y_{1}-a
.

Label all vertices in
T_{2}
that are adjacent to none in
Y_{1}
, similar to and including
c
, with the label (t2, old) first. Now every remaining vertex in
Y_{1}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
T_{2}
. So we can label via pairs.
\Box

Observation 6.14

The graph
G[Y_{i}\cup Y_{i+1}\cup T_{i+1}]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. Assume
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
, and
T_{2}
are non-empty. By P
, a vertex in
Y_{1}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
T_{2}
. By P
, a vertex in
Y_{1}
is also non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
Y_{2}
. However, a vertex in
T_{2}
may be non-adjacent to many vertices in
Y_{1}
. We will label these vertices in
T_{2}
first.

Label a vertex in
T_{2}
that is non-adjacent to some vertices in
Y_{1}
with the label (t2, new). For each of the vertices it is non-adjacent to in
Y_{1}
: label them (y1, new), label the vertex in
Y_{2}
that it is non-adjacent to (y2, new) if it exists, make the join between (t2, new) and (y2, new), between (y1, old) and (y1, new), and between (y2, old) and (y2, new). Then relabel (y2, new) to (y2, old) and relabel (y1, new) to (y1, old). Repeat for each vertex (t2, new) is non-adjacent to. Now make the join between vertices with labels (t2, old) and (t2, new) and relabel (t2, new) to (t2, old). Repeat with the next
T_{2}
vertex that is non-adjacent to some vertices in
Y_{1}
.

Label any remaining
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
non-adjacencies via pairs. Now the rest of the relations between the cliques are either a join or a co-join, for which labelling is trivial.
\Box

Lemma 6.15

If
Y_{i}
and
Y_{i+1}
are non-empty,
G[Y\cup T]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. Assume
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are non-empty. Then by P
, since
Y_{1}
is non-empty,
Y_{3}
and
Y_{5}
must be empty. Similarly, since
Y_{2}
is non-empty,
Y_{4}
and
Y_{6}
must also be empty. So of the sets
Y_{i}
, we have only
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
non-empty.

Suppose
T_{1}
is non-empty. Since
Y_{1}
and
T_{1}
are non-empty, by P
,
T_{3}
is empty. By P
,
T_{2}
and
T_{6}
are empty. By P
,
T_{4}
is also empty. So only
T_{5}
may be non-empty. By P
,
Y_{1}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{1}
. By Observations
and
, all other relations between
Y_{1},Y_{2},T_{1},T_{5}
are a co-join, so labelling is trivial. Thus
Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}\cup T_{1}\cup T_{5}
has bounded clique-width. This holds as long as

is non-empty, regardless of whether
T_{5}
is empty, since we have not used that
T_{5}
is non-empty in our argument.

Now suppose
T_{1}
is empty but
T_{2}
is non-empty. Since
Y_{2}
and

,
T_{4}
is empty. By P
,
T_{3}
is empty. By P
,
T_{5}
is also empty. So only
T_{6}
may be non-empty. Assume it is non-empty since otherwise, by P
6.14
, the graph has bounded clique-width. By Observations
and
,

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}
. By P
, since
T_{6}
is non-empty,
Y_{1}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}

. By P
,
Y_{2}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{2}
. So we can take the disjoint union of graphs
G[Y]
and

and then make the join between vertices with labels (y1, old) and (t2, old) and vertices with labels (y2, old) and (t2, old). Thus
Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}\cup T_{2}\cup T_{6}
has bounded clique-width.

Suppose both

and
T_{2}
are empty. Then the case when
T_{3}
is non-empty is symmetric to the case when
T_{2}
is non-empty and the case when
T_{4}
is non-empty is symmetric to the case when
T_{1}
is non-empty.

By P
,
T_{5}
and
T_{6}
may not both be non-empty at a time. The cases are symmetric, so without loss of generality suppose
T_{5}
is non-empty. By P
and
,

, so we need only take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
T_{5}
and
G[Y]
and we are done. Thus
Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}\cup T_{5}
has bounded clique-width.

Thus if
Y_{2}
is non-empty,
G[Y\cup T]
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

Observation 6.16

The graph
G[Y_{i}\cup Y_{i+3}\cup T_{i+1}]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. Assume
Y_{1}
,
Y_{4}
, and
T_{2}
are non-empty. By P
,
Y_{4}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{2}
. By P
, a vertex in
Y_{1}
is non-adjacent to at most two vertices in
T_{2}
. By P
, a vertex in
Y_{1}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
Y_{4}
and vice versa.

Similar to what is done in P
6.13
, label the vertices in
T_{2}
that are adjacent to none in
Y_{1}
first. Now every remaining vertex in
Y_{1}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
T_{2}
and vice versa. We can label the non-adjacent pairs of
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
and the adjacent pairs of
Y_{1}
and
Y_{4}
via rows. Then the remaining vertices of the sets are all either a join or a co-join with each other, for which labelling is trivial.

Thus
G[Y_{i}\cup Y_{i+3}\cup T_{i+1}]
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

Lemma 6.17

If
Y_{i}
and
Y_{i+3}
are non-empty,
G[Y\cup T]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. Assume
Y_{1}
and
Y_{4}
are non-empty. Then by P
,
Y_{2}
,
Y_{3}
,
Y_{5}
, and
Y_{6}
must all be empty. So of the sets
Y_{i}
, we have only
Y_{1}
and
Y_{4}
non-empty. By P
, a vertex in
Y_{1}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
Y_{4}
and vice versa.

Suppose
T_{1}
is non-empty. Since
Y_{1}
and
T_{1}
are non-empty, by P
,
T_{3}
is empty. By P
,
T_{2}
and
T_{6}
are empty. So only
T_{5}
may also be non-empty. By P
,
T_{4}
is also empty. If only
T_{1}
is non-empty, it is a join with
Y_{1}
and a co-join with
Y_{4}
, for which labelling is trivial.

The case when only
T_{5}
is non-empty is symmetric to P
6.16
and thus the graph has bounded clique-width in this case.

Suppose both
T_{1}
and
T_{5}
are non-empty. By P
, a vertex in
T_{1}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
T_{5}
and vice versa. Then we can label via rows, thus
Y_{1}\cup Y_{4}\cup T_{1}\cup T_{5}
has bounded clique-width.

Now suppose
T_{1}
is empty but
T_{2}
is non-empty. By P
6.16
, if only
T_{2}
is non-empty, the graph has bounded clique-width. By P
,
T_{3}
is empty. By P
,
T_{5}
is also empty. So only
T_{4}
and
T_{6}
may also be non-empty.

Suppose
T_{2}
and
T_{4}
are both non-empty. By P
,
Y_{1}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{2}
. By Observations
and
, all other relations between the sets
Y
and
T
are either a join or a co-join. So we can take the disjoint union of the graphs
G[T]
and
G[Y]
, then make the join between vertices with labels (y4, old) and (t4, old) and vertices with labels (y1, old) and (t2, old). Thus
Y_{1}\cup Y_{4}\cup T_{2}\cup T_{4}
has bounded clique-width.

Suppose
T_{2}
and
T_{6}
are both non-empty. Then by P
,
T_{4}
is empty and the graph is symmetric to
G[Y_{1}\cup Y_{4}\cup T_{1}\cup T_{5}]
. Thus the graph has bounded clique-width as explained above.

Now suppose both
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are empty. Then all other sets
T_{i}
are symmetric to the case of either
T_{1}
or
T_{2}
being non-empty.

Therefore, if
Y_{1}
and
Y_{4}
are non-empty, the graph
G[Y\cup T]
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

Lemma 6.18

The graph
G[Y\cup T]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. Since we can take any non-empty set
Y_{i}
to be
Y_{1}
, without loss of generality assume
Y_{1}
is non-empty. By Lemmas
6.15
and
6.17
and by P
, we may assume all other sets
Y_{i}
are empty.

Suppose
T_{2}
is empty. By Observations
,
, and
,
Y_{1}
is either a join or a co-join with all other sets
T_{i}
. Thus regardless of which sets
T_{i}
are non-empty, we can take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
Y_{1}
and
G[T]
, then make join between the sets
Y_{1}
and
T_{i}
as needed. Thus, if
T_{2}
is empty,
G[Y\cup T]
has bounded clique-width.

Suppose
T_{2}
is non-empty. By P
,
T_{1}
and
T_{3}
are empty. By P
,
T_{5}
is also empty. So only
T_{4}
and
T_{6}
may be non-empty. By P
6.13
, if only
T_{2}
is non-empty, the graph has bounded clique-width.

Suppose
T_{2}
and
T_{4}
are both non-empty. By P
,
T_{2}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{4}
. So we can take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
Y_{1}\cup T_{2}
and
T_{4}
, thus the graph has bounded clique-width.

The case when
T_{2}
and
T_{6}
are both non-empty is symmetric to the case when
T_{2}
and
T_{4}
are both non-empty.

Suppose
T_{2}
,
T_{4}
, and
T_{6}
are all non-empty. We know
T_{2}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

and

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{4}\cup T_{6}
. Then to label the graph, we need only take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
Y_{1}\cup T_{2}
and
T_{4}\cup T_{6}
. Thus the graph has bounded clique-width.

Therefore, the graph
G[Y\cup T]
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

6.2.6
The Graph
G[X\cup Y]

Lemma 6.19

The graph
G[X\cup Y]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. By Observations
,
, and
, all sets
Y_{i}
and
X_{i}
are either related by a join, a co-join, or if one is non-empty then the other is empty. Then to label
G[X\cup Y]
, first take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
G[X]
and
G[Y]
. Then for all
Y_{i}
and
X_{i}
, make the join between labels (yi, old) and (xi, old) as necessary to satisfy P
. Thus the graph
G[X\cup Y]
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

6.2.7
The Graph
G[T\cup X\cup Y]

For simplicity, we will examine the graph in terms of the sets
Y_{i}
. Since we can take any non-empty set
Y_{i}
to be
Y_{1}
, without loss of generality assume
Y_{1}
is non-empty. Then by P
, we have three possible combinations of sets
Y_{i}
:

Only one set
Y_{i}
is non-empty. Assume
Y_{1}
is non-empty.

Two consecutive sets
Y_{i}
and
Y_{i+1}
are non-empty. Assume
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are non-empty.

Two non-consecutive sets
Y_{i}
and
Y_{i+3}
are non-empty. Assume
Y_{1}
and
Y_{4}
are non-empty.

Lemma 6.20

If
Y_{i}
is non-empty, the graph
G[T\cup X\cup Y]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. Assume
Y_{1}
is non-empty. Then by P
,
X_{3}
and
X_{6}
are empty.

Then the only relationship between
Y_{1}
and any of the sets
X_{i}
or
T_{i}
that is not a join or a co-join is between
Y_{1}
and
T_{2}
. So if
T_{2}
is empty, take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
G[Y]
and
G[T\cup X]
, then make the join between the labelled vertices of the sets as necessary. Thus, if
T_{2}
is empty, the graph
G[Y_{1}\cup X\cup T]
has bounded clique-width.

Now suppose
T_{2}
is non-empty. Since we know
X_{3}
and
X_{6}
are empty, by Observations
and
,
T_{2}
forms either a join or a co-join with all non-empty sets
X_{i}
. Then when of the sets
T_{i}
only
T_{2}
is non-empty, we can take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
G[Y_{1}\cup T_{2}]
and
G[X]
, then make the join between labelled vertices of the sets as necessary. Thus,
G[Y_{1}\cup T_{2}\cup X]
has bounded clique-width.

Suppose
T_{2}
is not the only non-empty set
T_{i}
. By P
,
T_{1}
and
T_{3}
are empty. By P
,
T_{5}
is also empty. So only
T_{4}
and
T_{6}
may be non-empty.

Suppose
T_{2}
and
T_{4}
are both non-empty. Then by P
,
Y_{1}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}

. Since

is non-empty and by symmetry, by P
,

Lemma 6.22

If
Y_{i}
and
Y_{i+3}
are non-empty, the graph
G[T\cup X\cup Y]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. Assume
Y_{1}
and
Y_{4}
are non-empty. Then by P
,
X_{3}
and
X_{6}
are empty.

Then the only relationships between
Y_{1}
and
Y_{4}
and the other potentially non-empty sets that are not a join or a co-join are between
Y_{1}\cup T_{2}
and
Y_{4}\cup T_{5}
. Then if
T_{2}
and
T_{5}
are both empty, we can take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
Y_{1}\cup Y_{4}
and
G[T\cup X]
, then make the join between the labelled vertices of the sets as necessary. Thus if
T_{2}
and
T_{5}

Suppose

and
T_{5}
are not both empty. By P
, only one of
T_{2}
and
T_{5}
may be non-empty at a time. The cases are symmetric, so without loss of generality suppose

is non-empty.

Suppose

. By Observations
and
, since

and
X_{6}
are both empty, it forms a join or a co-join with all non-empty sets
X_{i}
. By Observations
and
,
Y_{1}
and
Y_{4}
also both form a join or a co-join with all non-empty sets
X_{i}
. Then we can take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
T_{2}\cup Y_{1}\cup Y_{4}
and
G[X]
, then make the join between the labelled vertices of the sets as necessary. Thus
G[T_{2}\cup Y\cup X]
has bounded clique-width.

Now consider when
T_{2}
is not the only non-empty set

. By Observations

T_{1}
and
T_{3}
are empty. So of the sets

and

Suppose

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

. By P

T_{4}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;Y_{4}
. By P
,
T_{4}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;Y_{1}
. We know
T_{2}
,
Y_{1}
, and
Y_{4}
all form either a join or a co-join with any non-empty sets
X_{i}
. So we can take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
G[T_{2}\cup Y_{1}\cup Y_{4}]
and
G[T_{4}\cup X]
, then make the join between labelled vertices of the sets as necessary. Thus,
G[T_{2}\cup T_{4}\cup Y\cup X]
has bounded clique-width.

The case when
T_{2}
and
T_{6}
are both non-empty is symmetric to the case when
T_{2}
and
T_{4}
are non-empty.

Suppose
T_{2}
,
T_{4}
, and
T_{6}
are all non-empty. We know
T_{2}
,
Y_{1}
, and
Y_{4}
all form either a join or a co-join with any non-empty sets
X_{i}
. We also know
T_{4}
and
T_{6}
form either a join or a co-join with all three sets
T_{2}
,
Y_{1}
, and
Y_{4}
. So we can take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
G[T_{2}\cup Y_{1}\cup Y_{4}]
and
G[T_{4}\cup T_{6}\cup X]
, then make the join between labelled vertices of the sets as necessary. Thus,
G[T_{2}\cup T_{4}\cup T_{6}\cup Y\cup X]
has bounded clique-width.

Therefore, when
Y_{1}
and
Y_{4}
are non-empty, the graph
G[T\cup X\cup Y]
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

6.3
Graphs containing a
C_{6}

The purpose of the previous results was to prove the following theorem:

Theorem 6.23

Let
G
be a
(claw,4K_{1},bridge,C_{4}
-
twin
)-free graph. If
G
contains a
C_{6}
, then
G
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
.
By Theorem
5.10
, we may assume that
G
contains no
C_{7}
. In Lemma
6.4
, we proved that if
G
consists of only the
C_{6}
and the sets
T_{i}
, then
G
has bounded clique-width. In Lemma
6.7
, we proved that if
G
consists of only the
C_{6}
and the sets
X_{i}
, then
G
has bounded clique-width. In Lemma
6.8
, we proved that if
G
consists of only the
C_{6}
and the sets
Y_{i}
, then
G
has bounded clique-width.

In Lemma
6.12
, we proved that if
G
consists of only the
C_{6}
and the sets
X_{i}
and
T_{i}
, then
G
has bounded clique-width. In Lemma
6.18
, we proved that if
G
consists of only the
C_{6}
and the sets
T_{i}
and
Y_{i}
, then
G
has bounded clique-width. In Lemma
6.19
, we proved that if
G
consists of only the
C_{6}
and the sets
X_{i}
and
Y_{i}
, then
G
has bounded clique-width. In Lemmas
6.21
and
6.22
, we proved that if
G
consists of the
C_{6}
and any combination of sets
T_{i}
,
X_{i}
, and
Y_{i}
, then
G
has bounded clique-width.

From Observations
6.1
and
6.3
, we know no other configuration of vertices is possible. Therefore, if
G
contains a
C_{6}
,
G
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

7
The case of the
C_{5}

By Lemma
3.1
, we will assume each of the sets
T_{i},X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i},R_{i}
is big, that is, they have at least
5
vertices. WLOG, we assume that if a set is non-empty, it is big.

In addition to the
claw
,
4K_{1}
,
bridge
, and
C_{4}
-
twin
, by Theorems
5.10
and
6.23
, we may also forbid the
C_{7}
and
C_{6}
as induced subgraphs.

Observation 7.1

Any vertex in
G
must belong to one of
H
,
T_{i}
,
X_{i}
,
Y_{i}
,
Z
, or
R
.

Proof
. Consider a vertex
a
that does not belong to the induced cycle
H
.

If
a
is not adjacent to any vertices of the cycle
H
, it is in
R
.

If
a
is adjacent to one vertex in the cycle
H
, say vertex
1
, then the set
\{1,5,2,a\}
induces a
claw
. Similarly if
a
is adjacent to two non-consecutive vertices in the cycle
H
, say vertices
1
and
3
, or if
a
is adjacent to three non-consecutive vertices, say
1
,
3
, and
4
, then the same set
\{1,5,2,a\}
induces a
claw
. Thus if
a
is adjacent to two vertices, it must be in
T_{i}
and if
a
is adjacent to three vertices, it must be in
X_{i}
.

If
a
is adjacent to four vertices of the cycle
H
, it is in
Y_{i}
. If
a
is adjacent to all five vertices of the cycle
H
, it is in
Z
.

Therefore, if
a
is not in
H
, it must belong to one of
T_{i}
,
X_{i}
,
Y_{i}
,
Z
, or
R
.
\Box

Observation 7.2

The sets
T_{i}
,
X_{i}
,
Y_{i}
,
Z
, and
R
are cliques for all
i
.

Proof
. If
T_{i}
contains non-adjacent vertices
a,b
then
\{i+1,a,b,i+2\}
is a
claw
. If
X_{i}
contains non-adjacent vertices
a,b
then
\{i+2,a,b,i+3\}
is a
claw
. Similarly, if
Y_{i}
contains non-adjacent vertices
a,b
then
\{i+3,i+4,a,b\}
is a
claw
and if
Z
contains non-adjacent vertices
a,b
, then
\{i+2,i+5,a,b\}
is a
claw
. If
R
contains non-adjacent vertices
a,b
, then
\{i,i+2,a,b\}
is a
4K_{1}
.
\Box

Observation 7.3

Y_{i}
are all empty.

Proof
. Suppose some
Y_{i}
contains two vertices
a,b
. Then the set
\{i,a,i+3,i+4,b\}
induces a
C_{4}
-
twin
. Then we have
|Y_{i}|\leq 1
for all
i
. This contradicts Lemma
3.1
. Thus, all
Y_{i}
must be empty.
\Box

7.1
Properties of the set
R

Observation 7.4

T_{i}\;\hbox to14.25pt{\vbox to14.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.12675pt\lower-7.12675pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.55554pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}

for all
i
.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in T_{1}
that is non-adjacent to a vertex
b\in R
, then the set
\{a,b,3,5\}
induces a
4K_{1}
.
\Box

Observation 7.5

If
R
is non-empty,
T_{i}
is empty for all
i
.

Proof
. By the assumption that sets are big, both
T_{1}
and
R
contain at least two vertices
a,b\in T_{1}
and
c,d\in R
. By Observation
7.5
,
a,b,c,d
are all adjacent. Then the set
\{1,2,a,b,c,d\}
induces a
bridge
.
\Box

Observation 7.6

X_{i}\;\hbox to14.25pt{\vbox to14.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.12675pt\lower-7.12675pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.55554pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;R
for all
i
.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in X_{1}
that is adjacent to a vertex
b\in R
, then the set
\{a,b,1,3\}
induces a
claw
.
\Box

Observation 7.7

Z\;\hbox to14.25pt{\vbox to14.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.12675pt\lower-7.12675pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.55554pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;R

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in Z
that is adjacent to a vertex
b\in R
, then the set
\{a,b,1,3\}
induces a
claw
.
\Box

Observation 7.8

The set
R
is empty.

Proof
. By Observations
7.3
,
7.5
,
7.6
and
7.7
, if
R
is non-empty then the graph
G
is disconnected. Since
R
is a clique, it has bounded clique-width. Then when
R
is removed, if
G
has bounded clique-width, so does
G\cup R
. Therefore, we need only consider the graph
G
when
R
is empty.
\Box

7.2
Properties of the set
Z

Observation 7.9

If
Z
is non-empty, then
T_{i}
is empty for all
i
.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in Z
that is adjacent to a vertex
b\in T_{i}
, then the set
\{a,b,3,5\}
induces a claw. So
Z\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{i}
. By the assumption that sets are big, there is another vertex
c\in T_{i}
and
d\in Z
, then the set
\{a,b,c,d,1,2\}
induces a
bridge
.
\Box

Observation 7.10

If
Z
is non-empty, then
X_{i}
is empty for all
i
.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in X_{i}
that is adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in Z
, then the set
\{a,2,4,5,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
. Then any vertex in
X_{i}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
Z
. Since by Lemma
3.1

Z
is big,
a
is non-adjacent to at least four vertices in
Z
. Then there will be two vertices
a,b\in X_{i}
which are non-adjacent to vertices
c,d\in Z
. Then the set
\{a,b,c,d,1,2\}
induces a
bridge
.
\Box

Observation 7.11

If
Z
is non-empty, then
G
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. If
Z
is non-empty, then the graph
G
consists of the
C_{5}
and the set
Z
. Since
Z
is a clique, labelling is trivial.
\Box

\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;X_{i+2}

P 64

If
X_{i}\neq\emptyset
, then
T_{i}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{i+2}

P 65

If a vertex in
X_{i}
is adjacent to vertices in
X_{i+2}
and in
T_{i+4}
, then its neighbours in
X_{i+2}\cup T_{i+4}
are a clique

P 66

If
X_{i}
,
X_{i+1}
,
T_{i+1}
are all non-empty, then
X_{i}\cup X_{i+1}\cup T_{i+1}
is a clique

7.4
Clique-width Labelling

7.4.1
The Graph
G[T]

Lemma 7.12

The graph
G[T]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. Since we can take any non-empty set
T_{i}
to be
T_{1}
, without loss of generality assume
T_{1}
is non-empty. If only two sets
T_{i}
are non-empty, by Observations
49
and
50
, they form either a co-join or a vertex in one set is adjacent to at most one vertex in the other set and can therefore be labelled via pairs. Thus, if only two sets
T_{i}
are non-empty, the graph
G[T]
has bounded clique-width.

Now consider when more than two sets
T_{i}
are non-empty. Suppose
T_{2}
is non-empty. Then by P
52
,
T_{3}
and
T_{5}
are empty. So only
T_{4}
may be non-empty.

By Property P
49
,
T_{1}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{2}
. By Property P
51
, a vertex in
T_{4}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
and vice versa. So we can label via pairs.

Any other combination of three sets
T_{i}
is symmetric to
T_{1}\cup T_{2}\cup T_{4}
. Therefore, the graph
G[T]
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

7.4.2
The Graph
G[X]

Lemma 7.13

The graph
G[X]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. Since we can take any non-empty set
X_{i}
to be
X_{1}
, without loss of generality assume
X_{1}
is non-empty. If any two sets
X_{i}
are non-empty, by Observations
53
and
54
, a vertex in the first set is either adjacent or non-adjacent to at most one vertex in the second set and thus the union of the sets can be labelled via pairs.

Now consider more than two non-empty sets
X_{i}
. Suppose
X_{2}
is non-empty. Then by Property P
55
,
X_{3}
and
X_{5}
are empty. So only
X_{4}
may be non-empty.

By Property P
53
, a vertex in
X_{1}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{2}
. By Property P
56
,
X_{1}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;X_{4}
and
X_{2}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;X_{4}
. So we can label
X_{1}\cup X_{2}
via pairs then take the disjoint union with
X_{4}
.

Any other combination of three sets
X_{i}
is symmetric to
X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup X_{4}
. Therefore,
G[X]
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

7.4.3
The Graph
G[T\cup X]

Lemma 7.14

If
X_{i}
and
X_{i+1}
are non-empty, the graph
G[T\cup X]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. Assume
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are non-empty. By Property P
53
, a vertex in
X_{1}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{2}
and vice versa. By Property P
55
,
X_{3}
and
X_{5}
are empty. So of the sets
X_{i}
, only
X_{4}
may also be non-empty.

Consider the sets
T_{i}
. By Property P
60
,
T_{1}
and
T_{3}
are empty.

Suppose
T_{2}
is non-empty. By Property P
66
,
T_{1}\cup X_{1}\cup X_{2}
is a clique. Thus if only
X_{1},X_{2}
and
T_{2}
are non-empty, the graph has bounded clique-width. We will refer to the clique formed by
X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup T_{2}
as
X_{1}^{*}
.

Suppose
X_{4}
is also non-empty. By Observations
59
and
56
,
X_{4}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;X_{1}^{*}
. Then if both
T_{4}
and
T_{5}
are empty, the graph consists of two disjoint cliques
X_{4}
and
X_{1}^{*}
, and labelling is trivial. Thus, we may assume
T_{4}
or
T_{5}
is non-empty.

Suppose
T_{4}
is also non-empty, so we have sets
X_{1},X_{2},X_{4},T_{2},T_{4}
non-empty. By Property P
57
,
T_{4}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;X_{4}
. By Property P
59
,
T_{4}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;X_{1}
. Since
X_{2}
is non-empty, by Property P
64
and symmetry,
T_{4}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{2}
. By Property P
58
, a vertex in
T_{4}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{2}
and vice versa. Then the graph
G[T_{4}\cup X_{1}^{*}]
satisfies Lemma
2.4
and thus has bounded clique-width. Then when
X_{4}
is non-empty, take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
G[T_{4}\cup X_{1}^{*}]
and
X_{4}
and make the join between vertices with labels (x4, old) and (t4, old). So this graph also has bounded clique-width.

The case when
T_{5}
is non-empty is symmetric to the case when
T_{4}
is non-empty. By Property P
61
and symmetry, both cannot be non-empty at the same time.

Now suppose that
T_{2}
is empty and only
T_{4}
of the sets
T_{i}
is non-empty, so we have sets
X_{1},X_{2},T_{4}
non-empty. Then
X_{1}\cup X_{2}
is no longer a clique. By Property P
53
, a vertex in
X_{1}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{2}
, and vice versa. By Property P
59
, there are no edges between
T_{4}
and
X_{1}
. By Property P
58
, a vertex in
T_{4}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{2}
. Thus we can label the vertices of
X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup T_{4}
via rows.

Since if
X_{4}
is also non-empty it forms a join with
T_{4}
and a co-join with
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, we can take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup T_{4}
and
X_{4}
then make the join between vertices with labels (x4, old) and (t4, old). Thus this graph has bounded clique-width.

Therefore if
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are non-empty, the graph
G[T\cup X]
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

Lemma 7.15

If
X_{i}
and
X_{i+2}
are non-empty, the graph
G[T\cup X]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. Assume
X_{1}
and
X_{3}
are non-empty. By Lemma
7.14
, we can assume no two consecutive sets
X_{i}
and
X_{i+1}
are non-empty. Thus
X_{2}
,
X_{4}
, and
X_{5}
are all empty. So we have only
X_{1}
and
X_{3}
non-empty. By Property P
54
, a vertex in
X_{1}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{3}
and vice versa.

Suppose
T_{1}
is non-empty. Then by Property P
61
,
T_{2}
is empty. By Property P
57
,
T_{1}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;X_{1}
. By Property P
59
,
T_{1}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;X_{3}
. So if only
T_{1}
is non-empty, take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
G[X]
and
T_{1}
, then make the join between vertices with labels (t1, old) and (x1, old). Thus
X_{1}\cup X_{3}\cup T_{1}
has bounded clique-width.

Suppose
T_{1}
is empty but
T_{3}
is non-empty. Then by Property P
61
,
T_{4}
is empty. By Property P
57
,

Suppose all three sets

are non-empty. Take the disjoint union of labelled graphs

The case when
T_{2}
is non-empty is symmetric to the case when
T_{3}
is non-empty. The case when
T_{4}
is non-empty is symmetric to the case when
T_{1}
is non-empty.

Therefore if
X_{1}
and
X_{3}
are non-empty, the graph
G[T\cup X]
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

Lemma 7.16

The graph
G[T\cup X]
has bounded clique-width.

Proof
. Since we can take any non-empty set
X_{i}
to be
X_{1}
, without loss of generality assume
X_{1}
is non-empty. If
X_{2}
or
X_{5}
are non-empty, refer to Lemma
7.14
. If
X_{3}
or
X_{4}
are non-empty, refer to Lemma
7.15
. So of the sets
X_{i}
, only
X_{1}
is non-empty.

If there is only one set
T_{i}
that is non-empty, by Observations
57
,
58
,
58
, and
59
, it forms either a join or co-join with
X_{1}
, or a vertex in
T_{i}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{1}
and vice versa, in which case we can label via rows. Thus
G[T\cup X]
has bounded clique-width.

Consider when
T_{1}
is non-empty. Then by Property P
61
,
T_{2}
is empty. By Property P
57
,
T_{1}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;X_{1}
.

Suppose
T_{1}
and
T_{3}
are non-empty. By Property P
64
,
T_{1}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{3}
. By Observations
58
and
58
, a vertex in
T_{3}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{1}
and vice versa. So label

via rows, take the disjoint union with labelled

, then make the join between vertices with labels (t1, old) and (x1, old). Thus

has bounded clique-width.

Suppose
T_{1}
and
T_{5}
are non-empty. By Property P
49
,
T_{1}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{5}
. By Observations

and

, a vertex in

Suppose

, and

Now consider when

is non-empty. By Property P
59
,
T_{4}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;X_{1}
.

Suppose
T_{1}
is also non-empty. By Property P
50
, a vertex in
T_{1}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
T_{4}
and vice versa. Then we can label
T_{1}\cup T_{4}
via pairs, take the disjoint union with labelled
X_{1}
, then make the join between vertices with labels (t1, old) and (x1, old).

By Property P
52
, one of
T_{3}
or
T_{5}
must be empty. The cases are symmetric, so without loss of generality assume
T_{3}
is non-empty. By Property P
49
,
T_{4}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{3}
. If
T_{1}
is empty, then we can take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
G[T_{3}\cup X_{1}]
and
T_{4}
. Thus
X_{1}\cup T_{3}\cup T_{4}
has bounded clique-width. If
T_{1}
is non-empty, then we can take the disjoint union of labelled graphs
G[T_{3}\cup X_{1}]
and
G[T_{1}\cup T_{4}]
labelled via pairs, then make the join between labels (t1, old) and (x1, old). Thus
X_{1}\cup T_{1}\cup T_{3}\cup T_{4}
has bounded clique-width

The case when
T_{2}
is non-empty is symmetric to the case when
T_{1}
is non-empty.

Therefore the graph
G[T\cup X]
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

7.5
Graphs containing a
C_{5}

The purpose of the previous results was to prove the following theorem:

Theorem 7.17

Let
G
be a
(claw,4K_{1},bridge,C_{4}
-
twin)
-free graph. If
G
contains a
C_{5}
, then
G
has bounded clique-width.

Proof.
By Theorems
5.10
and
6.23
,
we may assume that
G
contains no
C_{7}
and no
C_{6}
. In Lemma
7.12
, we proved that if
G
consists of only the
C_{5}
and the sets
T_{i}
, then
G
has bounded clique-width. In Lemma
7.13
, we proved that if
G
consists of only the
C_{5}
and the sets
X_{i}
, then
G
has bounded clique-width. In Lemma
7.16
, we proved that if
G
consists of the
C_{5}
and the sets
X_{i}
and
T_{i}
, then
G
has bounded clique-width. In Observation
7.11
, we showed that if the set
Z
is not empty, then the graph has bounded clique-width.

From Observations
7.1
and
7.3
, we know no other configuration of vertices is possible. Therefore, if
G
contains a
C_{5}
,
G
has bounded clique-width.
\Box

8
Proof of the main result

The purpose of this section is to synthesize the previous results to show that
(claw,4K_{1},bridge,C_{4}
-
twin)
-free graphs can be coloured in polynomial time using the structure results from Sections
5
,
6
, and
5
. We will prove the two theorems mentioned in Section
1
.

Theorem
1.2

Let
G
be a (
claw
,
4K_{1}
,
bridge
,
C_{4}
-twin)-free graph. Then one of the following holds.

(i)

G
is perfect.

(ii)

G
has bounded clique-width.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.
Let
G
be a (
claw
,
4K_{1}
,
bridge
,
C_{4}
-twin)-free graph. We may assume that
G
is not perfect, for otherwise we
are done. Suppose
G
contains an odd hole
H
. Since
G
is
4K_{1}
-free,
H
is a
C_{5}
or
C_{7}
. From Theorems
7.17
and
5.10
, we know that
G
has bounded clique-width. Now, we may assume that
G
contains an odd antihole
F
. Since the
C_{5}
and its complement are isomorphic, if
F
has five vertices, by Theorem
7.17
, we are done. So we can assume
F
is of size at least
7
. Let
\{1,2,3,4,5,6,7,\dots,k\}
be the vertices of
F
where the non-edges are
(i,i+1)
with the subscript taken modulo
k
. Then the set
\{1,6,2,5,3\}
induces a
C_{4}
-
twin
, a contradiction.
\Box

Theorem
1.1

There is a polynomial-time algorithm to colour a
(claw,4K_{1},bridge,C_{4}
-
twin)
-free graph.

Proof
. Let
G
be a
(claw,4K_{1},bridge,C_{4}
-
twin)
-free graph. If
G
is perfect, then by the result of Hsu from
[
15
]
,
G
can be coloured in polynomial time, we are done. So by Theorem
1.2
,
G
has bounded clique-width. Then we can colour
G
in polynomial time using the algorithm from
[
18
]
.
\Box

9
Conclusion and open problems

The complexity of vertex colouring for
(claw,4K_{1})
-free graphs is one of only three open problems for four-vertex forbidden induced graphs. The problem has been solved for some subclasses with additional forbidden subgraphs. In this paper, we proved colouring is polynomial-time solvable for the subclass of
(claw,4K_{1},bridge,C_{4}
-
twin)
-free graphs by proving graphs in this class are either perfect or have bounded clique-width and can thus be coloured using either Hsu’s algorithm from
[
15
]
or Rao’s algorithm from
[
18
]
.

The
C_{4}
-
twin
was used in six observations in the case of the
C_{6}
and six observations in the case of the
C_{5}
, for a total of twelve observations. We did not use the
C_{4}
-
twin
in the case of the
C_{7}
. Thus, we believe that omitting the
C_{4}
-
twin
entirely and showing that
(claw,4K_{1},bridge)
-free graphs have bounded clique-width is possible. Thus we conjecture the following:

Conjecture 9.1

Let G be a
(claw,4K_{1},bridge)
-free graph. Then one of the following holds.

•

G is perfect.

•

G has bounded clique-width.

Since we have shown this is true when the graph contains a
C_{7}
, it would only be a matter of proving it for when the graph contains a
C_{5}
or a
C_{6}
. Since the
C_{4}
-
twin
is not used in every observation regarding the properties of the graphs when they contain a
C_{5}
or a
C_{6}
, many of the observations in this paper hold regardless of whether the
C_{4}
-
twin
is no longer included as a forbidden induced subgraph. New observations would need to be made only in place of the twelve occurrences of the
C_{4}
-
twin
. It may be possible to prove it while omitting an investigation of the case of the
C_{6}
as well, since the
C_{6}
was only added to make the case of the
C_{5}
more manageable. It may be possible to investigate the
C_{5}
without forbidding the
C_{6}
.

To conclude, we note that the complexity of colouring
(claw,4K_{1})
-free graphs in general has remained an open problem, along with the two other open problems for four-vertex forbidden induced subgraphs outlined in
[
16
]
and
[
10
]
, namely
(claw,4K_{1},co
-
diamond)
-free and
(4K_{1},C_{4})
-free graphs.

In this appendix, we give proofs for properties (in the three tables) that were not given in the main body of the paper.

Appendix B
Properties of sets in the case of the
C_{6}

P

If
T_{i}\not=\emptyset
, then
T_{i+1}=\emptyset
and vice versa. By symmetry, if
T_{i}\not=\emptyset
, then
T_{i+5}=\emptyset
and vice versa.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in T_{1}
that is adjacent to a vertex
b\in T_{2}
, then the set
\{1,a,b,3,4,5,6\}
induces a
C_{7}
. Conversely, if
a
and
b
are not adjacent, then the set
\{a,b,4,6\}
induces a
4K_{1}
.
\Box

P

A vertex in
T_{i}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
T_{i+2}
and vice versa. By symmetry, a vertex in
T_{i}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
T_{i+4}
and vice versa.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in T_{1}
that is adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in T_{3}
, then the set
\{a,2,3,b,c\}
induces a
C_{4}
-
twin
.
\Box

P

If
T_{i}\not=\emptyset
, then
T_{i+3}=\emptyset
.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in T_{1}
that is not adjacent to a vertex
b\in T_{4}
, then the set
\{a,3,b,6\}
induces a
4K_{1}
. So we must have
T_{1}\;\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{4}
. Since
T_{1}
and
T_{4}
are big, there are two vertices
c\in T_{1}
and
d\in T_{4}
such that
\{a,b,c,d\}
is a clique. Then the set
\{1,2,a,c,b,d\}
induces a
bridge
.
\Box

P

If a vertex in
T_{i}
is adjacent to a vertex in
T_{i+2}
and a vertex in
T_{i+4}
, those vertices must also be adjacent. By symmetry, the same is true if a vertex in
T_{i+2}
is adjacent to a vertex in
T_{i}
and a vertex in
T_{i+4}
and if a vertex in
T_{i+4}
is adjacent to a vertex in
T_{i}
and a vertex in
T_{i+2}
.

Proof
. Suppose there is a vertex
a\in T_{1}
that is adjacent to two vertices
b\in T_{3}
and
c\in T_{5}
. If
b
and
c
are not adjacent, then the set
\{a,2,b,c\}
induces a
claw
.
\Box

P

A vertex in
X_{i}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{i+1}
and vice versa. By symmetry, a vertex in
X_{i}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{i+5}
and vice versa.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in X_{1}
that is non-adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in X_{2}
, then we claim that every other vertex
d\in X_{1}
must be adjacent to exactly one of
\{b,c\}
. Otherwise if
d
is non-adjacent to both, the set
\{a,d,2,3,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
or, if
d
is adjacent to both, the set
\{1,a,d,2,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
. WLOG, assume
d
is adjacent to
b
. Then by the assumption that
X_{1}
is big, there is another vertex
e\in X_{1}
that is also adjacent to
b
. Then the set
\{c,4,b,3,e,d\}
induces a
bridge
.
\Box

P

A vertex in
X_{i}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{i+2}
and vice versa. By symmetry, a vertex in
X_{i}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{i+4}
and vice versa.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in X_{1}
that is adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in X_{3}
, then we claim that every other vertex
d\in X_{1}
must be adjacent to exactly one of
\{b,c\}
. Otherwise if
d
is non-adjacent to both, then the set
\{d,2,a,3,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
or if
d
is adjacent to both then the set
\{1,2,a,d,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
. WLOG, assume
d
is adjacent to
b
. Then by the assumption that
X_{1}
is big, there is another vertex
e\in X_{1}
that is also adjacent to
b
. Then the set
\{d,e,b,3,c,4\}
induces a
bridge
.
\Box

P

X_{i}\;\hbox to14.25pt{\vbox to14.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.12675pt\lower-7.12675pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;X_{i+3}
for all
i
.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in X_{1}
that is adjacent to a vertex
b\in X_{4}
, then the set
\{a,b,1,3\}
induces a
claw
.
\Box

P

There are no 3 consecutive non-empty sets
X_{i}
.

Proof
. Suppose
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}
are non-empty. Since the sets
X_{i}
are all big, by Property P
, there are vertices
a\in X_{1}
and
c\in X_{3}
that are not adjacent. Since the set
X_{2}
is big, by Property P
, there are many vertices
b\in X_{2}
that are adjacent to both
a
and
c
. Then the set
\{a,2,b,3,c,4\}
induces a
bridge
.
\Box

P

A vertex in
Y_{i}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
Y_{i+1}
and vice versa. By symmetry, a vertex in
Y_{i}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
Y_{i+5}
and vice versa.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in Y_{1}
that is non-adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in Y_{2}
, then we claim that every other vertex
d\in Y_{1}
must be adjacent to exactly one of
\{b,c\}
. Otherwise, if
d
is non-adjacent to both, then the set
\{d,a,2,3,c,b\}
induces a
bridge
or, if
d
is adjacent to both, then the set
\{a,1,d,2,c,b\}
induces a
bridge
. WLOG, assume
d
is non-adjacent to
b
and adjacent to
c
. Then by the assumption that
Y_{1}
is big, there is another vertex
e\in Y_{1}
that is also non-adjacent to
b
and adjacent to
c
. Then since
b
is non-adjacent to both
d
and
e
, by symmetry, every other vertex in
Y_{2}
must be adjacent to exactly one of
\{d,e\}
. But
c
is adjacent to both. Contradiction.
\Box

P
a

A vertex in
Y_{i}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
Y_{i+2}
and vice versa. By symmetry, a vertex in
Y_{i}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
Y_{i+4}
and vice versa.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in Y_{1}
that is adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in Y_{3}
, then the set
\{a,b,c,1,6\}
induces a
C_{4}
-
twin
.
\Box

P

If
Y_{i}\not=\emptyset
, then
Y_{i+2}=\emptyset
and vice versa. By symmetry, if
Y_{i}\not=\emptyset
, then
Y_{i+4}=\emptyset
and vice versa.

Proof
. Consider a vertex
a\in Y_{1}
. By Property P
a,
a
is non-adjacent to some vertices
b,c\in Y_{1}
. Consider a second vertex
d\in Y_{1}
. If
d
is non-adjacent to both
b
and
c
, the set
\{a,d,3,4,b,c\}
induces a bridge. This along with Property P
a means
d
must be adjacent to exactly one of
\{b,c\}
. This is true of every vertex in
Y_{1}-a
. Then since
Y_{1}
is big, at least one of
b,c
is adjacent to more than one vertex in
Y_{1}
, contradicting Property P
a. Therefore, both sets cannot be non-empty.
\Box

P

A vertex in
Y_{i}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
Y_{i+3}
and vice versa.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in Y_{1}
that is adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in Y_{4}
, then we claim that every other vertex
d\in Y_{1}
must be adjacent to exactly one of
\{b,c\}
. Otherwise, if
d
is non-adjacent to both, then the set
\{d,3,a,4,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
or, if
d
is adjacent to both, then the set
\{2,3,a,d,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
. WLOG, assume
d
is non-adjacent to
b
and adjacent to
c
. Then by the assumption that
Y_{1}
is big, there is another vertex
e\in Y_{1}
that is also non-adjacent to
b
and adjacent to
c
. Then since
b
is non-adjacent to both
d
and
e
, by symmetry, every other vertex in
Y_{4}
must be adjacent to exactly one of
\{d,e\}
. But
c
is adjacent to both. Contradiction.
\Box

P

A vertex in
Y_{i}
is non-adjacent to at most two vertices in
T_{i+1}
.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in Y_{1}
that is non-adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in T_{2}
, then we claim that every other vertex
d\in Y_{1}
must be adjacent to exactly one of
\{b,c\}
. Otherwise, if
d
is non-adjacent to both, then the set
\{a,d,2,3,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
or, if
d
is adjacent to both, then the set
\{a,1,2,d,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
. Then if
a
is non-adjacent to a third vertex
e\in T_{2}
, any other vertex
d
must be adjacent to exactly one of
\{b,c\}
, one of
\{b,e\}
, and one of
\{e,c\}
, which is not possible.
\Box

P

If a vertex in
Y_{i}
is non-adjacent to two vertices in
T_{i+1}
, one of those vertices must be adjacent to all of
Y_{i}
while the other must be non-adjacent to all of
Y_{i}
.

Proof
. If there is a vertex in
a\in Y_{1}
that is non-adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in T_{2}
, then we know from Property P
that every other vertex in
Y_{1}
is adjacent to exactly one of
\{b,c\}
. We will show that it must be the same one.

Consider
d,e\in Y_{1}
. Let
d
be adjacent to
b
and non-adjacent to
c
while
e
is non-adjacent to
b
and adjacent to
c
. Now consider another vertex
f\in Y_{1}
. We know
f
must also be adjacent to exactly one of
\{b,c\}
. WLOG, assume it is adjacent to
b
and non-adjacent to
c
. Then the set
\{b,c,e,d,f\}
induces a
C_{4}
-
twin
.
\Box

P
a

A vertex in
Y_{i}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{i+2}

Y_{i}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{i+5}

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in Y_{1}
that is non-adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in X_{3}
, then we claim that every other vertex
d\in Y_{1}
must be adjacent to exactly one of
\{b,c\}
. Otherwise, if
d
is non-adjacent to both, then the set
\{a,d,3,4,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
or, if
d
is adjacent to both, then the set
\{a,2,d,3,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
. WLOG, assume
d
is non-adjacent to
b
and adjacent to
c
. Then by the assumption that
Y_{1}
is big, there is another vertex
e\in Y_{1}
that is also non-adjacent to
b
and adjacent to
c
. Then the set
\{d,e,c,4,b,5\}
induces a
bridge
.
\Box

.

Proof
. If there are vertices
a\in T_{1}
and
b\in T_{2}
, then we may choose
c\in T_{3}
such that by Property P
50
,
c
is one of the many vertices in
T_{3}
that is non-adjacent to
a
. By Property P
49
,
a
and
b
are not adjacent and
b
and
c
are not adjacent. Then the set
\{a,b,c,5\}
induces a
4K_{1}
.
\Box

P
53

A vertex in
X_{i}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{i+1}
and vice versa. By symmetry, a vertex in
X_{i}
is non-adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{i+4}
and vice versa.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in X_{1}
that is non-adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in X_{2}
, then we claim that every other vertex
d\in X_{1}
must be adjacent to exactly one of
\{b,c\}
. Otherwise if
d
is non-adjacent to both, then the set
\{a,d,2,3,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
or, if
d
is adjacent to both, then the set
\{a,1,d,2,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
. Then two vertices
d,e\in X_{1}
must be adjacent to the same vertex of
\{b,c\}
. WLOG, assume they are both adjacent to
b
and not adjacent to
c
. Then the set
\{e,d,b,3,c,4\}
induces a
bridge
.
\Box

P
54

A vertex in
X_{i}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{i+2}
and vice versa. By symmetry, a vertex in
X_{i}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{i+3}
and vice versa.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in X_{1}
that is adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in X_{3}
, then the set
\{1,a,b,5,c\}
induces a
C_{4}
-
twin
.
\Box

P
55

There are no 3 consecutive non-empty sets
X_{i}
.

Proof
. Consider a vertex
a\in X_{1}
. From Property P
53
, we may choose
b\in X_{2}
such that it is one of the many vertices in
X_{2}
that are adjacent to
a
. From Property P
53
and Property P
54
, we may choose
c\in X_{3}
such that
c
is one of the many vertices in
X_{3}
that is non-adjacent to
a
and adjacent to
b
. Then the set
\{a,2,b,3,c,4\}
induces a
bridge
.
\Box

P
56

If
X_{i}
and
X_{i+1}
are non-empty, then

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.55554pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

. Consider a vertex

for all

. By symmetry,
T_{i}\;\hbox to14.25pt{\vbox to14.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.12675pt\lower-7.12675pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

. If there is a vertex

P
58

A vertex in
T_{i}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{i+1}
. By symmetry, a vertex in
T_{i}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
X_{i+3}
.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in T_{1}
that is adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in X_{2}
, then we claim that every other vertex
d\in T_{1}
must be adjacent to exactly one of
\{b,c\}
. Otherwise, if
d
is non-adjacent to both, then the set
\{d,1,a,2,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
, or, if
d
is adjacent to both, then the set
\{d,a,b,c,3,4\}
induces a
bridge
. If both
b
and
c
have neighbours
d,e\in T_{1}-a
respectively, then there will be another vertex
f\in T_{1}
that is adjacent to one of
\{b,c\}
. Then the set
\{b,c,d,e,f\}
induces a
C_{4}
-
twin
. So WLOG,
b
is adjacent to all of
T_{1}-a
, while
c
is non-adjacent to all of
T_{1}-a
. So
b
is adjacent to
d,e\in T_{1}
. Then the set
\{d,e,b,2,c,3\}
induces a
bridge
.
\Box

P
58
vv

A vertex in
X_{i+1}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
T_{i}
. By symmetry, a vertex in
X_{i+3}
is adjacent to at most one vertex in
T_{i}
.

Proof
. If there is a vertex
a\in X_{2}
that is adjacent to two vertices
b,c\in T_{1}
, then we claim that every other vertex
d\in X_{2}
must be adjacent to exactly one of
\{b,c\}
. Otherwise, if
d
is non-adjacent to both, then the set
\{d,3,a,2,b,c\}
induces a
bridge
, or, if
d
is adjacent to both, then the set
\{b,c,a,d,3,4\}
induces a
bridge
. Then by the assumption that
X_{2}
is big, at least one of
\{b,c\}
is adjacent to more than one vertex in
X_{2}
. Contradiction to Property P
58
.
\Box

59

T_{i}\;\hbox to14.25pt{\vbox to14.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.12675pt\lower-7.12675pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

.

P
60

One of
\{T_{i},X_{i},X_{i+1}\}
must be empty. By symmetry, one of
\{T_{i+2},X_{i},X_{i+1}\}
must be empty.

Proof
. If there are vertices
a\in T_{1}
,
b\in X_{1}
, we may choose
c\in X_{2}
such that it is one of the many vertices that, by Property P
58
, is not adjacent to
a
and, by Property P

, is adjacent to
b
. By Property P
57
,
a
and
b
are adjacent. Then the set
\{a,1,b,2,c,3\}
induces a
bridge
.
\Box

P
61

One of
\{T_{i},X_{i},T_{i+1}\}
must be empty.

Proof
. Consider vertices
a\in T_{1}
,

and

. By Property P
57
,
a
and
b
are adjacent and
b
and

are also adjacent. By Property P
49
,
a
and

are not adjacent. Then the set
\{a,1,b,2,c,3\}
induces a

.

is non-empty,

}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;X_{i+2}
. By symmetry, if

is non-empty,

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;X_{i+2}
.

Proof
. Consider vertices

,

, and

. By Property P

,

and

are adjacent. If

is not adjacent to

, then if

is adjacent to
b
, the set

induces a

. So if

is not adjacent to

, then it is not adjacent to

.

Suppose

is adjacent to

. Then by Property P
58
,
a
is not adjacent to any other vertex in
X_{3}
. Suppose
c
is adjacent to
b
, since otherwise the observation holds. Since
X_{3}
is big and by Property P
58
, there are two vertices
d,e\in X_{3}
that are not adjacent to
a
. By the previous argument, since
d
and
e
are not adjacent to
a
, they are also not adjacent to
b
. Then the set
\{a,b,c,3,d,e\}
induces a
bridge
. Therefore,
c
cannot be adjacent to
b
, so the observation holds.
\Box

P
64

If
X_{i}
is non-empty,

}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;T_{i+2}

Proof
. Consider vertices
a\in X_{1}
,
b\in T_{1}
, and
c\in T_{3}
. By Property P
57
,
a
and
b
are adjacent. Suppose
c
is not adjacent to
a
. If
b
and
c
are adjacent, then the set
\{3,a,b,c,2\}
induces a
C_{4}
-
twin
. Now suppose
c
is adjacent to
a
. By Property P
58
, there is another vertex
d\in X_{1}
that is not adjacent to
c
. If
b
and
c
are adjacent, then the set
\{b,d,3,c,2\}
induces a
C_{4}
-
twin
. Thus, if a vertex
a\in X_{1}
exists, any vertices
b\in T_{1}
and
c\in T_{3}
must be non-adjacent.
\Box

P
65

If a vertex in
X_{i}
is adjacent to vertices in
X_{i+2}
and in
T_{i+4}
, then its neighbours in
X_{i+2}\cup T_{i+4}
are a clique.

Proof
. Consider vertices
a\in X_{1}
,
b\in X_{3}
and
c\in T_{5}
. If
a
is adjacent to both
b
and
c
but
b
and
c
are not adjacent, then the set
\{a,2,b,c\}
induces a
claw
. So if a vertex in
X_{1}
is adjacent to a vertex in
X_{3}
and one in
T_{5}
, those vertices in
X_{3}
and
T_{5}
must also be adjacent.
\Box

P
66

If
X_{i}
,
X_{i+1}
,
T_{i+1}
are all non-empty, then
X_{i}\cup X_{i+1}\cup T_{i+1}
is a clique.

Proof
. Consider the case when the sets
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
, and
T_{2}
are non-empty. By Property P
57
,
T_{2}\cup X_{2}
and
T_{2}\cup X_{1}
are cliques. By Property P
62
,
X_{1}\cup X_{2}
is also a clique. Thus, the union of the three sets forms a clique.
\Box
